Naked Singularity Censoring with Anisotropic Apparent Horizon
Xinliang An

Abstract
. Employing the Einstein-scalar field system, we demonstrate an approach for proving high co-dimensional nonlinear instability of naked-singularity solutions as constructed by Christodoulou in [ 18 ] . We further investigate the censorship of Christodoulou’s naked singularity and show that a tiny anisotropic perturbation arising from the outgoing characteristic initial data would lead to the emergence of an anisotropic apparent horizon, which covers and censors the naked singularity. Our approach advances the hyperbolic short-pulse method by not requiring the aid of additional large parameters, by permitting the use of initial perturbations for the shear tensor and the derivative of scalar field to be with finite B ​ V BV and C 0 C^{0} norms , and by allowing the initial perturbation to be arbitrarily small in scale-critical norms. New elliptic arguments based on non-perturbative methods are also developed.



1.
Introduction

In 1994 Christodoulou
[
18
]
constructed his famous naked-singularity solution in
3+1
dimensions for the Einstein-scalar field system

\begin{split}R_{\mu\nu}-&\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu},\\
T_{\mu\nu}=&D_{\mu}\phi D_{\nu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}D^{\lambda}\phi D_{\lambda}\phi.\end{split}

(1.1)

Here
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{3+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a real-valued scalar function and
(\mathcal{M},g)
is the
3+1
dimensional spacetime. The stability or instability of these naked singularities is closely related to the
weak cosmic censorship

[
34
,
20
]
:

Conjecture 1
.

(Weak cosmic censorship conjecture).
For generic asymptotically flat initial data, the maximal development of Einstein’s field equations possesses a complete future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
and hides the possibly formed singularities in a (black hole) region causally disconnected from
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

The above is one of the greatest open problems in classic general relativity. When Penrose formulated the original weak cosmic censorship conjecture in
[
34
]
, the caveat generic was not included. Christodoulou’s example in
[
18
]
surprisingly shows that naked singularity could form in the evolution of Einstein’s field equations. Within spherical symmetry, for the Einstein-scalar field system (
1.1
), by a celebrated series of works
[
16
,
17
,
19
]
Christodoulou proved that the initial datum leading to the naked-singularity formation is rare, and for generic initial data the possibly formed singularities are indeed hidden in the trapped (black hole) region. This motivates him to add the term
generic
for Conjecture
1
in
[
20
]
.

Christodoulou’s proofs in
[
16
,
17
,
19
]
reveal novel mathematical and physical properties of the spherically symmetric gravitational collapse. For the initial datum leading to naked singularity, in
[
19
]
he identified
2
instability mechanisms and deduced co-dimensional
2
instability. These instability theorems show that each rare initial datum, which leads to naked singularity formation, is always associated with
2
-dimensional perturbations and each of the perturbations would give rise to a trapped surface and hence a black hole formation. Here a trapped surface is a
2
-sphere in
\mathcal{M}
with both null expansions negative.

In above arguments, the spherically symmetric perturbation required in
[
19
]
is a bit restrictive, since it imposes a global condition in all angles. Can we relax this requirement by only placing a small anisotropic-in-angle perturbation? If it is possible, this may also significantly raise the co-dimensions of the instability theorems. In this article, we give such an anisotropic result. In particular, we prove that
for Christodoulou’s constructed naked singularity in
[
18
]
, by imposing a tiny perturbation from any angle, we can trigger a trapped region and an apparent horizon to censor the naked singularity.
Compared to Christodoulou’s instability argument that the singularity is covered by later-formed trapped surfaces, here we further show that the naked singularity is censored by an
achronal
apparent horizon emerging from it. Subjected to a general anisotropic perturbation, the naked singularity in
[
18
]
is not naked even locally. It is completely censored for observers outside the apparent horizon.

1.1.
Main Theorem

Here we use the double-null foliation for the spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g)
.
Let
u
and
\underline{u}
be optical functions increasing towards the future and satisfy the eikonal equations

g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u\partial_{\nu}u=0,\quad g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\underline{u}\partial_{\nu}\underline{u}=0.

Set
H_{u}
to be the level sets of
u
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
to be the level sets of
\underline{u}
. And we require that
H_{u}
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
correspond to the outgoing and incoming null cones, respectively. The intersections of
H_{u}
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
are topologically 2-spheres and are denoted by
S_{u,\underline{u}}
. We further fix a frame
e_{1},e_{2}
tangent to
S_{u,\underline{u}}
and denote
\nabla
to be the angular derivative on it.
In this paper, we employ the double null coordinates
(u,\underline{u},\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
for
(\mathcal{M},g)
, where
\partial/\partial\theta^{1}
and
\partial/\partial\theta^{2}
are tangential to
S_{u,\underline{u}}
.
1
1

1

See Chapter 1 and Chapter 2 in
[
21
]
for a detailed discussion of the double null coordinates.
For
A,B=1,2
, the spacetime metric
g
takes the form

g=-2\Omega^{2}(du\otimes d\underline{u}+d\underline{u}\otimes du)+g\mkern-9.0mu/_{AB}(d\theta^{A}-d^{A}d\underline{u})\otimes(d\theta^{B}-d^{B}d\underline{u}).

Here
\Omega(u,\underline{u},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
is the lapse function,
g\mkern-9.0mu/_{AB}
is the induced metric on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, and
A,B=1,2
. For notational simplicity, in this article we also use
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
to denote
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
. With the help of
\Omega
, we choose the null pair
e_{3},e_{4}
in this article as

e_{3}=\Omega^{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial u},\quad\quad e_{4}=\Omega^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\underline{u}}+d^{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{A}}\right)

with
d^{A}
obeying
d^{A}=0
on
H_{-1}
. And it holds

g(e_{3},e_{3})=0,\quad g(e_{4},e_{4})=0,\quad g(e_{3},e_{4})=-2.

Define

\chi_{AB}:=g(D_{e_{A}}e_{4},e_{B})

and set
\hat{\chi}
to be its traceless part, i.e., the shear tensor. In this article, we first prove

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider the characteristic initial value problem for the Einstein-scalar field system (
1.1
). Assigning Christodoulou’s naked-singularity initial data in
[
18
]
along
\underline{H}_{0}
with
-1\leq u\leq 0
and prescribing perturbed initial data along
H_{-1}
satisfying

\sum_{i\leq 5,j\leq 3}\underline{u}^{j}\|(\partial_{\underline{u}})^{j}\nabla^{i}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}+\underline{u}^{j}\|(\partial_{\underline{u}})^{j}\nabla^{i}\nabla_{4}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\leq B,

(1.2)

then for each
B
there exist a sufficient small
\delta=\delta(B)
and the Einstein-scalar field system admits a unique regular solution in the spacetime region
(u,\underline{u},\omega)
satisfying
0\leq\underline{u}\leq\delta
and
\underline{u}\leq|u|\Omega^{2-\tilde{\delta}}(u,0)\leq 1
with
0<\tilde{\delta}\ll 1
a fixed constant.

Moreover, if we further assume

\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)+[\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)-\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,0,\omega)]^{2}d\underline{u}^{\prime}=f(\omega,\underline{u})\underline{u},

(1.3)

with
f(\omega,\underline{u})
obeying

0\leq f\leq 1\mbox{ on }\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(0,\delta]\quad\mbox{ and }\quad f(\cdot,\underline{u})\geq m\mbox{ on }B_{p}(\epsilon)

(1.4)

for a point
p\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
and constants
m\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,\pi/2)
, then within the solved hyperbolic region, there exists a unique MOTS (marginally outer trapped surface)
M_{\underline{u}}
on each
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
with
0<\underline{u}\leq\delta
and the collection of MOTS
\{M_{\underline{u}}\}
emerges and censors the central singularity and forms an achronal apparent horizon.

For the shear tensor and the derivative of scalar field, the above theorem prescribes initial perturbation along
H_{-1}^{[0,\delta]}
with a finite
BV
norm satisfying (
1.3
). Our next theorem further allows initial perturbation along
H_{-1}^{[0,\delta]}
to be with finite
C^{0}
norm.

Theorem 1.2
.

The above statements in Theorem
1.1
still hold, if we replace the requirements for
f(\omega,\underline{u})
in (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) by

f(\omega,\underline{u})=g(\underline{u})\tilde{f}(\omega,\underline{u})\,\,\mbox{ with }\,\,g(\underline{u})\approx[\ln(\ln\frac{1}{\underline{u}})]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\,\mbox{ obeying }\,\,\lim_{\underline{u}\rightarrow 0^{+}}g(\underline{u})=0,

and
\tilde{f}(\omega,\underline{u})
satisfying
0\leq\tilde{f}\leq 1\mbox{ on }\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(0,\delta],\,\,\tilde{f}(\cdot,\underline{u})\geq m\mbox{ on }B_{p}(\epsilon)
for a point
p\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
with constants
m\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,\pi/2)
.

O
S_{-(\tilde{\underline{u}}a)^{1-\mu^{*}},\tilde{\underline{u}}}
H_{-1}(u=-1)
S_{-(\delta a)^{1-\mu^{*}},\delta}
\underline{H}_{\delta}(\underline{u}=\delta)
\underline{H}_{0}(\underline{u}=0)
\underline{H}_{\tilde{\underline{u}}}(\underline{u}=\tilde{\underline{u}})

Remark 1
.

To set the above-mentioned
C^{0}
perturbation, we can let

|\hat{\chi}|(-1,\underline{u},\omega)+|\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)-\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,0,\omega)|=[\ln(\ln\frac{1}{\underline{u}})]^{-\frac{1}{4}}\cdot\tilde{f}(\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}}

with
\tilde{f}(\omega)
being a smooth function depending only on
\omega
. By Proposition
13.4
, we have

\|[\ln(\ln\frac{1}{\underline{u}})]^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}([0,\delta])}\lesssim[\ln\frac{1}{\delta}]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll 1.

Hence, with Theorem
1.2
, to trigger the censoring of naked-singularity, we only need
the scale-critical perturbation of
g
in
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}{(H_{-1}^{[0,\delta]}})}
norm, i.e.,

\mbox{the }\|\cdot\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}{(H_{-1}^{[0,\delta]}})}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\underline{u}}([0,\delta])}+\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\underline{u}}\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\omega}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\mbox{ norm}

of
\hat{\chi}(-1,\underline{u},\omega)
and
\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)-\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,0,\omega)
to be of order
[\ln\frac{1}{\delta}]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll 1
. And
as
\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}
, we allow the size of the perturbation to shrink to
0
.

Remark 2
.

Compared with the previous short-pulse results in the scale-critical regime, here we don’t need the additional large parameter
a
and we can view that the parameter
a
is set to be

a=a(B)=1
. This is an important
advance
for the short-pulse method invented in
[
21
]
by Christodoulou with
a=\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gg 1
and generalized in
[
2
]
and
[
4
]
with
a=a(B)\gg 1
by the author and Luk, and by the author, respectively. With the aid of this parameter
a
, we could consider the strength of the short pulse to be of order
a^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The larger the short-pulse perturbation, the easier to form a trapped surface. A novel insight of this paper is that, via employing the instability nature of the interior naked-singularity solution, to form an anisotropic trapped surface and the corresponding apparent horizon, we can set the upper bound of the short-pulse perturbation to be of order
a(B)^{\frac{1}{2}}=1
, and according to (
1.4
) we could even set the size of short pulse to be a universal small number
m
.
As pointed out in Remark
1
, we can further choose its size to be
g(\underline{u})
with
g(\underline{u})\rightarrow 0
as
\underline{u}\rightarrow 0^{+}
. The perturbation allowed can be arbitrarily small measured at the scale-critical level.
This is markedly different from
[
2
]
by the author and Luk, and
[
6
]
by the author and Han, where the constant
a=a(B)
has to be a large parameter and
the fact
1/a\ll 1
is utilized crucially throughout
[
2
,
6
]
to control the nonlinear terms. This paper contains other hyperbolic and elliptic advancements. To capture the key features of the anisotropic instability mechanism, we also develop quite a few new ingredients. These are summarized in Section
1.4
.

Remark 3
.

In this paper, all the estimates still hold if we restore sharp
a
-weights and set
a=a(B)
to be a parameter greater than or equal to
1
. In particular, we allow
a=a(B)\gg 1
, but we do not need
a\gg 1
to close the arguments. To be consistent with
[
2
,
6
]
and to contain more analytic information for further use, in below sections we work under the following assumptions for initial perturbation:

\sum_{i\leq 5,j\leq 3}\underline{u}^{j}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|(\partial_{\underline{u}})^{j}\nabla^{i}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}+\underline{u}^{j}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|(\partial_{\underline{u}})^{j}\nabla^{i}\nabla_{4}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\leq B,

(1.5)

and

\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)+[\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)-\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,0,\omega)]^{2}d\underline{u}^{\prime}=f(\omega,\underline{u})\underline{u}a.

(1.6)

Setting
a=a(B)=1
, we then retrieve assumptions (
1.2
), (
1.3
).

Remark 4
.

In below, we also give a stereoscopic perspective, which demonstrates naked-singularity censoring subject to an anisotropic perturbation. Note that, as portrayed in the following picture, the anisotropic apparent horizon censors the naked singularity even locally. No local observer before the apparent horizon can detect the naked singularity. What we prove in this paper is a more precise statement than showing tiny trapped surfaces formed around
O
.

Anisotropic Perturbation
along

u=-1
S_{-1,0}

Figure 2.

Our next result further exhibits a high co-dimensional nonlinear instability:

Theorem 1.3
.

Consider the space of prescribed initial data for
\hat{\chi}(-1,\underline{u},\omega)
and
\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)
along
u=-1
with
\underline{u}\geq 0
. With
|\hat{\chi}|(-1,0,\omega)=0,\,\,\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)=\sqrt{2}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{\mu^{*}}}\cdot\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{4}
corresponding to their values of Christodoulou’s naked-singularity solution in
[
18
]
, then for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we can find functions
f_{1},f_{2},...,f_{k}
and
g_{1},g_{2},...,g_{k}
, which are continuous with respect to the
\underline{u}
variable and smooth with respect to the
\omega
variable, such that the below prescribed initial data for
|\hat{\chi}|
and
\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi
with
\underline{u}\geq 0

\begin{split}&\bigg(|\hat{\chi}|(-1,\underline{u},\omega),\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)\bigg)\\
=&\bigg(0+\lambda_{1}f_{1}+\lambda_{2}f_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}f_{k},\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)+\lambda_{1}^{\prime}g_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime}g_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}^{\prime}g_{k}\bigg)\end{split}

would lead to the trapped surface and the apparent horizon formation when

\sum_{1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}+\sum_{1}^{k}{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}\neq 0\quad\mbox{ with }\quad\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

Moreover, if it holds

\begin{split}&\bigg(0+\lambda_{1}f_{1}+\lambda_{2}f_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}f_{k},\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)+\lambda_{1}^{\prime}g_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime}g_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}^{\prime}g_{k}\bigg)\\
=&\bigg(0+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}f_{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}f_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\tilde{\lambda}_{k}f_{k},\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{\prime}g_{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}^{\prime}g_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\tilde{\lambda}_{k}^{\prime}g_{k}\bigg),\end{split}

then it renders

\lambda_{i}=\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad 1\leq i\leq k.

Hence, we may say that, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, Christodoulou’s naked-singularity solution has at least co-dimension
2k
nonlinear instability subject to outgoing continuous characteristic perturbations at
(-1,0,\omega)
.

Remark 5
.

In Section
13
, we obtain
two
instability theorems. With respect to the shear tensor and the derivative of the scalar field, Theorem
13.1
proves instability subject to
perturbations with finite
BV
norms
and Theorem
1.3
proves instability subject to perturbations
with finite
C^{0}
norms
. In
[
2
,
3
,
6
]
, to trigger the short pulse, BV perturbations for
\partial_{\underline{u}}g\mkern-9.0mu/|_{S_{-1,0}}
are prescribed. In
[
2
,
3
,
6
]
, when
\underline{u}\leq 0
, the shear tensor
\hat{\chi}
vanishes, and when
\underline{u}>0
, the shear tensor
\hat{\chi}
obeys a large lower bound related to the large parameter
a
. For this article, in Section
13.1
we first prove Theorem
13.1
, which is consistent with
[
2
,
3
,
6
]
by imposing the BV perturbations. In Section
13.2
, we further allow the perturbations to be
C^{0}
. Additional gains in both hyperbolic and elliptic parts enable us to do so. Detailed discussions are provided in Section
13
.

Remark 6
.

Our above theorems prove arbitrarily large co-dimensional nonlinear instability for a naked-singularity solution. This is not reported before and we spot the importance of the anisotropic arguments. Together with Remark
1
, we can see that this paper contains a desired scale-critical nonlinear instability result.

Remark 7
.

The proofs of above two theorems and the approach we demonstrate here have the potential to be generalized to other Einstein field equations. For the hyperbolic part, we employ
\Omega(u,0)\rightarrow 0
as
u\rightarrow 0
. For the elliptic part, we utilize
\Omega\underline{\omega}(u,0)=-\partial_{u}\log\Omega(u,0)/2>0
, which also holds for Rodnianski-Shlapentokh-Rothman’s naked-singularity in
[
35
]
for the Einstein vacuum equations.

Our proofs of above two theorems are rooted in recent progress on trapped surface formation and apparent horizon emergence. In below, we review these developments.

1.2.
Formation of Trapped Surface

With a 589-page proof, Christodoulou
[
21
]
proved the first trapped-surface-formation result for Einstein’s equations with no symmetry. As depicted in Figure
3
below, Christodoulou considered a characteristic initial-value problem for the Einstein vacuum equations in the shadowed region. Minkowskian initial data are prescribed along the incoming null cone
\underline{H}_{0}
and short-pulse initial data are prescribed along the outgoing null cone
H_{-1}
. Along
H_{-1}
, denoting
\chi
to be its associated outgoing null second fundamental form and setting
\hat{\chi}
to be its traceless part, in 2008 Christodoulou proved the following
2
2

2

In
[
21
]
Christodoulou’s short-pulse initial data are prescribed at past null infinity. Here we focus on its correspondence in a finite region.
:

H_{-1}(u=-1)
\underline{H}_{\delta}(\underline{u}=\delta)
\underline{H}_{0}(\underline{u}=0)
H_{u_{*}}

Figure 3.

Theorem

(Christodoulou
[
21
]
)
.

Set
\underline{H}_{0}
to coincide with a backward light cone in the Minkowskian spacetime for
-1\leq u\leq 0
. For every
B>0
and
-1<u_{*}<0
, there exists a
\delta=\delta(B,u_{*})>0
sufficiently small such that the following holds: for
0\leq\underline{u}\leq\delta
, if the prescribed
\hat{\chi}
on
H_{-1}
satisfies

\sum_{i\leq 5,\,j\leq 3}\delta^{\frac{1}{2}+j}\|(\partial_{\underline{u}})^{j}\nabla^{i}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\leq B

(1.7)

with
\nabla
being the angular derivative on
S_{-1,\underline{u}}
, then the solution to Einstein vacuum equations remains regular in the spacetime region
-1\leq u\leq u_{*}
,
0\leq\underline{u}\leq\delta
. Furthermore, if the below lower-bound assumption also satisfies

\inf_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\delta}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)\,d\underline{u}^{\prime}\geq M_{*}>2|u_{*}|,

(1.8)

then the
2
-sphere
S_{u_{*},\delta}
is a trapped surface.

Using similar upper and lower bounds as in
[
21
]
, extensions of Theorem
Theorem
via introducing the signatures for short pulse and for decay rates were obtained in
[
29
]
and
[
1
]
, respectively. The trapped surfaces formed are of radius close to
1
.

An important result was later obtained in
[
28
]
. Via deforming the double null foliation and solving a quasilinear elliptic inequality, the lower bound assumption in
[
21
]
is vastly relaxed to allow fully anisotropic initial data.

Theorem

(Klainerman-Luk-Rodnianski
[
28
]
)
.

Suppose that the initial data for Einstein vacuum equations satisfy the condition (
1.7
) in Theorem
Theorem
and the lower bound assumption

\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\delta}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)\,d\underline{u}^{\prime}\geq M_{*}>0.

Then, for sufficiently small
\delta
, a trapped surface forms in the evolution.

We also remark that, with a more geometric approach, Le in
[
30
]
gave a different proof of the main theorem in
[
28
]
.

Via introducing two more large parameters
a,b
satisfying
1\ll b\leq a^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and designing a new scale-critical short-pulse ansatz, a work
[
2
]
relaxed the upper bound assumption in
[
21
]
.

Theorem

(An-Luk
[
2
]
)
.

Prescribe Minkowskian data along
\underline{H}_{0}
with
-1\leq u\leq 0
. For a fixed
\delta
, for every
B
, there exist
a_{0}=a_{0}(B)
and
b_{0}=b_{0}(B)
sufficiently large such that the following hold: for any
a
and
b
verifying
a_{0}\leq a\leq\delta^{-1}
and
b_{0}\leq b\leq a^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, if prescribed
\hat{\chi}
along
H_{-1}
with
0\leq\underline{u}\leq\delta
satisfies

\sum_{i\leq 5,j\leq 3}\delta^{j}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla^{j}_{e_{4}}\nabla^{i}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\leq B,

(1.9)

then the Einstein vacuum equations admit a unique regular solution in the spacetime region
-1\leq u\leq-b\delta a^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
0\leq\underline{u}\leq\delta
. Moreover, if we further require

\inf_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\delta}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)d\underline{u}^{\prime}\geq 4\delta a,

then
S_{-\delta a,\delta}
is the formed
2
-dimensional trapped surface.

Later, via employing only the signature for decay rates and a spacetime rescaling, a reproof and an extension of Theorem
Theorem
to the past null infinity was obtained in
[
4
]
for the case
b=a^{\frac{1}{2}}
by the author.

Remark 8
.

The scale-critical bounds in
[
2
]
inspire the hyperbolic estimates of this article. However, there are at least three major differences:

(1)

In
[
2
]
the formed trapped surface is
S_{-\delta a,\delta}
and it is in the approximately self-similar regime. The designed short-pulse hierarchy and the hyperbolic estimates are all consistent with the considerations of scaling criticality. This is the underlying reason that in
[
4
]
the author can give an alternative proof in the far field regime and very few borderline terms appear. In this article, the trapped surface is located around
S_{-(\delta a)^{1-\mu^{*}},\delta}
with
0<\mu^{*}<1
and it is not in the approximately self-similar regime. Hence, designing a suitable hierarchy and closing the bootstrap arguments are more delicate. We need to divide the spacetime into two regions and deal with many new borderline terms. Moreover, finding a proper order to conduct the estimates for recovering the bootstrap constants is crucial. The newly designed hierarchy is given in Section
2.4
and the order of estimates is explained in Section
2.6
.

(2)

To carry out the hyperbolic estimates in
[
2
]
and
[
4
]
, in the spacetime region considered, we apply the below inequality ubiquitously

\frac{\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}\leq\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\ll 1.

The above inequality hinges on the largeness of the additional parameter
a
. While in this article our conclusions do not depend on the largeness of
a
and we can set
a=1
, which advances the short-pulse method.

(3)

In this paper, we can even set the short-pulse to be small in scale-critical norms. To fulfill the requirement (
13.15
) in Theorem
13.3
, we can choose

|\hat{\chi}(-1,\underline{u},\omega)|+|\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)-\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,0,\omega)|\approx[\ln(\ln\frac{1}{\underline{u}})]^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

Note that the function
[\ln(\ln 1/\underline{u})]^{-\frac{1}{4}}\rightarrow 0
as
\underline{u}\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Hence, we can let
|\hat{\chi}(-1,\underline{u},\omega)|
and
\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)-\nabla_{4}\phi(-1,0,\omega)
to be absolutely continuous functions. Their BV norms in
\underline{u}
variable (and other scale-critical norms) can be set small. While in all previous trapped surface formation works outside of spherical symmetry, the BV perturbation of
|\hat{\chi}(-1,\underline{u},\omega)|
in
\underline{u}
variable requires to be large.

1.3.
Emergence of Apparent Horizon

Besides trapped surface formation, the rise of the apparent horizon in the gravitational collapse of Einstein’s field equations was proved in
[
3
]
by the author and in
[
6
]
by the author and Han. In
[
3
]
, the following result was proved

Theorem

(An
[
3
]
)
.

Under the same initial-data assumption as in Theorem
Theorem
, the Einstein vacuum equations admit a unique regular solution in the region
0<\underline{u}\leq\delta
and
-1\leq u\leq-b\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}
. In addition, assuming

\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)d\underline{u}^{\prime}=f(\omega,\underline{u})\underline{u}a\quad\mbox{for each }0<\underline{u}\leq\delta,

(1.10)

with
f(\omega,\underline{u})
being a smooth function in
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(0,\delta]
satisfying
20/21\leq f(\omega,\underline{u})\leq 22/21
.
Then there
exists a unique
MOTS
M_{\underline{u}}
along each
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}

(0<\underline{u}\leq\delta)
. Furthermore, requiring

a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\underline{u}}}^{j}\nabla^{j}_{e_{4}}\nabla^{i}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\leq B\mbox{ for and }i,j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

then the MOTSs
\{M_{\underline{u}}\}
together form a smooth apparent horizon.

Theorem

(An-Han
[

.

With the same prescribed initial data as in Theorem
Theorem

0<\underline{u}\leq\delta
, if we require
a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\underline{u}}}^{j}\nabla^{j}_{e_{4}}\nabla^{i}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{-1,\underline{u}})}\leq B

i,j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}

\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}|\hat{\chi}|^{2}(-1,\underline{u}^{\prime},\omega)d\underline{u}^{\prime}=f(\omega,\underline{u})\underline{u}a,

(1.11)

with
f(\omega,\underline{u})
satisfying

0\leq f\leq 1\quad\text{on }\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(0,\delta]\mbox{ and }f(\cdot,\underline{u})\geq m\quad\text{on }B_{p}(\epsilon)

for a point
p\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
and constants
m\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,\pi/2)
. Then in a large region of each
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
with
0<\underline{u}\leq\delta
, there

MOTS
M_{\underline{u}}
and the collection of MOTS
\{M_{\underline{u}}\}
emerge from the central singularity and form a smooth apparent horizon. Furthermore, if we require
|\underline{u}\partial_{\underline{u}}f(\omega,\underline{u})|\leq a^{-\frac{1}{3}}
, then the apparent horizon is a spacelike hypersurface, hence a dynamical horizon.

\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}

Remark 9
.

The proof in
[
6
]
takes two steps:

-

deriving apriori estimates via working through details of Moser’s iteration and Schauder’s estimates in the order of

C^{0}\rightarrow C^{0,\alpha}\rightarrow C^{1,\frac{1}{3}}\rightarrow C^{2,\frac{1}{3}}\mbox{ with }0<\alpha\ll 1;

-

conducting the method of continuity based on the explicit structure of the linearized operator.

Compared with
[
6
]
, in this article the geometry along the initial incoming cone is completely different and the main terms in the elliptic equation for
R(\underline{u},\omega)
(with
u=R(\underline{u},\omega)
being the MOTS along
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
) differentiate, which raises extra challenges. In
[
6
]
we employ the ansatz
R=\underline{u}ae^{-\widetilde{\phi}}
to translate the elliptic equation for
R(\underline{u},\omega)
into an elliptic equation for
\widetilde{\phi}
. In this article, we design and utilize two forms of ansatz:

(1)

R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}e^{-(1-\mu^{*})\widetilde{\phi}}
. We apply this form of
R(\underline{u},\omega)
to perform the apriori estimates via Moser’s iteration and Schauder’s estimates.

(2)

R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}\check{\phi}^{\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{\mu^{*}}}
. We employ this ansatz to eliminate the additional nonlinear borderline term (rising from naked-singularity initial data) in the elliptic equation of
R(\underline{u},\omega)
and to conduct the method of continuity for the corresponding new linearized equation.

1.4.
Difficulties and New Ingredients in the Proof

To prove Theorem
1.1
, we divide the considered spacetime region into two parts:

-

the exterior
\{(u,\underline{u},\omega)|\,-1\leq u\leq u_{1}\,\mbox{ and }\,0\leq\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq|u_{1}|\Omega^{2-\tilde{\delta}}(u_{1},0)\}
,

-

the interior
\{(u,\underline{u},\omega)|\,\,\,\,\,\,u_{1}\leq u\leq 0\,\mbox{ and }\,0\leq\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq|u|\Omega^{2-\tilde{\delta}}(u,0)\}
.

Here
|u_{1}|
is a small constant to be fixed later. For notational simplicity, in the rest of this article, we set constant
\tilde{\delta}
to obey
0<\tilde{\delta}\ll 1
. In reality, if needed we can choose
\tilde{\delta}
to be any number satisfying
0<\tilde{\delta}<1/2
and the below proof still works.

1.4.1.
Hyperbolic Part

Compared with
[
2
]
by An-Luk, here more challenges arise. We list some of them and state several strategies:

-

For the position of MOTS along each
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
, in contrast to the results in
[
2
,
4
,
6
]
, where the MOTS lies around
u=-\underline{u}a
, in this paper the MOTS is around
u=-(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}
with
0<\mu^{*}<1
. The hyperbolic estimates in
[
2
,
4
,
6
]
are consistent with asymptotically self-similar bounds and crucially rely on

\frac{\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}\lesssim\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\ll 1

with
a
being a large parameter. While here the MOTS is not located in the asymptotically self-similar regime, and to obtain a desired instability result we would allow general (tiny) anisotropic perturbations and would set the parameter
a
to be of size
1
. These bring many new difficulties, especially on how to design and prove the short-pulse hierarchy when
|u|
is small.

-

In this paper, we generalize our constructed ansatz in
[
2
,
4
]
by inserting extra
|u|
weights to the hierarchy. Employing the fact

\frac{1}{2}\Omega(u,0)\leq\Omega(u,\underline{u},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\leq 2\Omega(u,0)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\Omega^{2}(u,0)=|u|^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}},

the suitable
|u|
weights are added via imposing the corresponding
\Omega
weights. Especially, the
\Omega
-weight top-order energy estimates and top-order elliptic estimates are critical for our proof. In the energy estimates, because of the additional
\Omega
weights, basic integration-by-part and pairing arguments are revised. In comparison to
[
2
]
, the top-order elliptic estimates are more subtle. As shown in Section
4
and Section
7
, enhanced estimates for
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 4}\|u^{i}\nabla^{i}(\Omega\mbox{tr}\chi)\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}
,
\|u^{6}\nabla^{5}(\Omega\mbox{tr}\chi)\|_{L^{2}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}
,
precise estimates involving borderline terms for

\|u^{5}\nabla^{5}\underline{\eta}\|_{L^{2}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}
,
\|u^{6}\nabla^{5}\eta\|_{L^{2}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}
,
\|u^{5}\nabla^{5}(\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi},\hat{\underline{\chi}})\|_{L^{2}_{\underline{u}}L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}
are derived and are employed in an essential way. Subtle structures of the Einstein-scalar field system are explored.

-

When bounding the nonlinear terms in the interior region where
u_{1}\leq u\leq 0
, we utilize the inequality

\frac{\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|\Omega^{\frac{3}{2}}}\leq\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{\delta}}(u,0)=|u|^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{\delta}}\leq|u_{1}|^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{\delta}}\ll 1,

(1.12)

where
0<\tilde{\delta}\ll 1
and
|u_{1}|\ll 1
. Notice that the largeness of
a
is no longer required, but the power of
\Omega
has to be discreetly tracked.

-

To conduct energy estimates for the scalar field, to derive energy estimates for curvature components and to obtain elliptic estimates, we encounter terms, which only obey the upper bound

\frac{\underline{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{4}}}{|u|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Omega}=\bigg(\frac{\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|\Omega^{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By contract to (
1.12
), this quotient is no longer
\lesssim 1
. The terms satisfying only this bound serve as the new
borderline
terms to be estimated. We then add the weights
\big(1+\frac{\underline{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{4}}}{|u|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Omega}\big)
to the bootstrap assumptions. Finding the correct order to retrieve the constants (which do not depend on other bootstrap assumptions) in these borderline estimates is decisive.
Additional
\big(1+\frac{\underline{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{4}}}{|u|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Omega}\big)
factors are incorporated in the top-order bootstrap assumptions for
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5,2}(\underline{\eta})
,
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5,2}(\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi},\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi})
,
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5,2}(\Omega\hat{\underline{\chi}},\hat{\underline{\chi}})
,
\mathcal{S}(\nabla_{A}\phi)
,
\underline{\mathcal{S}}(\nabla_{3}\phi)
,
\mathcal{R}(K,\check{\sigma})
and
\underline{\mathcal{R}}(\underline{\beta})
listed in Section
2.4
. To improve the bootstrap assumption for each of these terms, the correctly designed order of arguments and enhanced estimates are vitally employed.

-

Compared with An-Luk
[
2
]
, there are extra borderline terms arising from
\Omega\underline{\omega}
and
\partial_{u}\phi
. Along
\underline{u}=0
, they satisfy

\Omega\underline{\omega}(u,0)=\frac{\mu^{*}}{4(1-\mu^{*})}\cdot\frac{1}{|u|}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\partial_{u}\phi(u,0)=\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}\frac{1}{|u|}.

They behave similarly to
\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}
and cause multiple new borderline terms.

-

To carry out energy estimates, besides
\check{\sigma}
defined in
[
2
]
, we also use renormalized quantities
\beta_{A}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi

(equivalent to
\beta_{A}-\frac{1}{2}R_{4A}
)
and
\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi

(equivalent to
\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}R_{3A}
)

to combine contributions of
\nabla_{3}\beta_{A}
,
\nabla_{3}R_{4A}
or
\nabla_{4}\underline{\beta}_{A}
,
\nabla_{4}R_{3A}
in null Bianchi equations (
2.13
) together
.
3
3

3

A similar idea was used in
[
5
]
by An-Athanasiou for the Einstein-Maxwell system.
Furthermore, we also introduce and employ the renormalized Gaussian curvature
K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi
.
This new renormalization comes from the below identity for the Weyl curvature

\nabla^{A}W_{A434}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}R_{43}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}R_{44}-\frac{1}{12}\nabla^{A}R(g_{A3}g_{44}-g_{A4}g_{43}).

It gives

\nabla_{4}(\rho-\frac{1}{4}R_{43}-\frac{1}{12}R)=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{A}W_{A434}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{3}R_{44}+\mbox{non-top-order terms}

with
\rho=W_{3434}/4
. Further calculations via applying the constraint equation imply that

\rho-\frac{1}{4}R_{43}-\frac{1}{12}R=-(K-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{4}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}.

For using vanishing initial data along
\underline{H}_{0}
, we then utilize the renormalized Gaussian curvature
K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi
to carry out the energy estimates.
In our estimates, some hidden structures of the Einstein-scalar field system are revealed.

1.4.2.
Elliptic Part

Because of the naked-singularity initial data, here the key structure of the main elliptic equation for MOTS is quite different from its counterpart in
[
6
]
. In this article, we design and utilize two forms of ansatz:

-

R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}e^{-(1-\mu^{*})\widetilde{\phi}}
. This is a generalization of
[
6
]
, where
R=\underline{u}ae^{-\widetilde{\phi}}
. We use this form to construct the anisotropic trapped surfaces and to conduct Moser’s iteration and Schauder’s estimates as in
[
6
]
.

-

R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}\check{\phi}^{\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{\mu^{*}}}
. The ansatz here is
new
and is
indispensable
for this paper. We employ this form to carry out the key continuity method for the new linearized equation to solve for the MOTS.

In below, we will give a more comprehensive explanation on the utilization of these two forms.

To construct the trapped and untrapped barriers and to derive the regularity estimates, we employ the ansatz
R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}e^{-(1-\mu^{*})\widetilde{\phi}}
. We observe that

-

Owning to Christodoulou’s initial data, the constructions of the multi- and single-valley anisotropic trapped surfaces as in
[
6
]
and
[
28
]
still hold. One can see the details in Section
9
.

-

For the apriori regularity estimates, we find that the approach developed in
[
6
]
by An-Han is robust enough and can be adapted to this new setting. Consider the main elliptic equation (
10.1
):

\begin{split}0=&\Delta_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}+1-\frac{1}{2}f(\underline{u},\omega)e^{\widetilde{\phi}}-4(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\underline{\omega}|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}\\
\end{split}

\begin{split}&-(1+\frac{R}{2}\Omega\mbox{tr}_{g}\underline{\chi})(1-\mu^{*})|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}+2\gamma^{ij}\eta_{i}\partial_{j}\widetilde{\phi}\\
&+[\frac{1}{2(1-\mu^{*})}R\Omega^{-1}\mbox{tr}_{g}\chi-1+\frac{1}{2}f(\underline{u},\omega)e^{\widetilde{\phi}}]\\
&-2(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}\cdot\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}.\end{split}

On the right side, besides the main terms
\Delta_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}+1-\frac{1}{2}f(\underline{u},\omega)e^{\widetilde{\phi}}
we denote

\displaystyle F_{1}:=

\displaystyle-4(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\underline{\omega}|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2},

\displaystyle F_{2}:=

\displaystyle-(1+\frac{R}{2}\Omega\mbox{tr}_{g}\underline{\chi})(1-\mu^{*})|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}+2\gamma^{ij}\eta_{i}\partial_{j}\widetilde{\phi}

\displaystyle+[\frac{1}{2(1-\mu^{*})}R\Omega^{-1}\mbox{tr}_{g}\chi-1+\frac{1}{2}f(\underline{u},\omega)e^{\widetilde{\phi}}]

\displaystyle-2(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}\cdot\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}.

In
[
6
]
we deal with the elliptic equation in the same form as above. But in
[
6
]
we get
|R\Omega\underline{\omega}|\leq|u|\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}/|u|^{2}=\underline{u}a^{\frac{1}{2}}/|u|\leq 1/a^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll 1
. Hence the
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
terms obey
|F_{1}|+|F_{2}|\ll|D\tilde{\phi}|^{2}+1
. While in this paper, we only have
|R\Omega\underline{\omega}|\lesssim|u|\cdot 1/|u|=1
and it only holds
|F_{1}|+|F_{2}|\leq|D\tilde{\phi}|^{2}+1
. This
-4(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\underline{\omega}|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}
later serves as the main term in the linearization arguments. Luckily, as pointed out one line below
(4.3)
in
[
6
]
, the approach developed in
[
6
]
via Moser’s iteration is robust enough to cover the case

|F_{1}|+|F_{2}|\leq c(|D\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}+1)\quad\mbox{ with }\quad c=1.

Hence it can still be applied here to obtain the apriori regularity estimates.

The new ansatz
R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}\check{\phi}^{\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{\mu^{*}}}
plays a vital role in linearization arguments.

-

If we proceed with the ansatz
R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}e^{-(1-\mu^{*})\widetilde{\phi}}
. The main elliptic equation for MOTS is then reduced to

0=\Delta_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}+1-\frac{1}{2}f(\omega,\underline{u})e^{\widetilde{\phi}}-4(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\underline{\omega}|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}+\mbox{small terms}.

In
[
6
]
, since
R\Omega\underline{\omega}
is small, the main terms in above equation are the first three terms on the right and the continuity argument via linearization can be carried out accordingly (without troubles from the
|\nabla_{\gamma}\widetilde{\phi}|^{2}
term). While in this paper, due to the naked-singularity initial data, we have

-4(1-\mu^{*})R\Omega\underline{\omega}(u,\underline{u},\omega)\approx-4(1-\mu^{*})R\cdot\frac{\mu^{*}}{4(1-\mu^{*})}\cdot\frac{1}{|u|}=-4\mu^{*}

being of size
1
. It is no longer negligible. Here, by introducing and employing the new ansatz
R=(\underline{u}a)^{1-\mu^{*}}\check{\phi}^{\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{\mu^{*}}}
, we eliminate this quadratic gradient borderline term and the equation of MOTS becomes

0=\Delta_{\gamma}\check{\phi}-\mu^{*}\check{\phi}+\frac{\mu^{*}}{2}f(\omega,\underline{u})\check{\phi}^{1-\frac{1}{\mu^{*}}}+\mbox{ small terms}.

This enables us to complete the method of continuity and to construct the unique MOTS along each
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
in the considered spacetime region.

We then consider the position of the MOTS.

-

By the elliptic arguments in Section
9
, along each
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
we have that the MOTS is bounded in between by an untrapped surface located at
u=-(2\underline{u}a/3)^{1-\mu^{*}}
and by a trapped surface lying within

-(\underline{u}a\cdot m\epsilon^{2})^{1-\mu^{*}}\leq u\leq-(\underline{u}a\cdot m\epsilon^{\tau+2})^{1-\mu^{*}}

with
\tau>2.2
. Note that, along the inner hypersurface
u=-(\underline{u}a\cdot m\epsilon^{\tau+2})^{1-\mu^{*}}
, it holds

\Omega(u,0)^{\frac{2}{\mu^{*}}}=|u|\Omega^{2}(u,0)=|u|^{\frac{1}{1-\mu^{*}}}=\underline{u}a\cdot m\epsilon^{\tau+2}.

(1.13)

To guarantee that this hypersurface is located within the hyperbolic existence region, we require
\underline{u}\leq|u|\Omega^{2-\tilde{\delta}}(u,0)
, which is equivalent to
|u|\Omega^{2}(u,0)\geq\underline{u}\Omega^{\tilde{\delta}}(u,0)
. Thus, we need

\underline{u}a\cdot m\epsilon^{\tau+2}=|u|\Omega^{2}(u,0)\geq\underline{u}\Omega^{\tilde{\delta}}(u,0).

(1.14)

The above inequality is equivalent to
m\epsilon^{\tau+2}\geq a^{-1}\Omega^{\tilde{\delta}}(u,0)
. Back to (
1.14
), together with (
1.13
) we need

m\epsilon^{\tau+2}\geq a^{-1}(\underline{u}a\cdot m\epsilon^{\tau+2})^{\frac{\mu^{*}\tilde{\delta}}{2}}.

(1.15)

Hence, if we let
\underline{u}
satisfy (
1.15
), then the apparent horizon constructed in this article stays in the obtained hyperbolic existence region. Moreover, to fix the small parameter
|u_{1}|
, we only need to require

|2\underline{u}a/3|^{1-\mu^{*}}\leq|u_{1}|\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\Omega(u_{1},0)=|u_{1}|^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}\ll 1.

(1.16)

By setting
\underline{u}
to be sufficiently small, clearly, we can choose
|u_{1}|
verifying (
1.16
). In addition, if we set
a=1
as in the statement of Theorem
1.1
, we would deduce all the elliptic arguments in the interior region.

1.4.3.
Instability Results.

Our proven anisotropic trapped surface and apparent horizon formation criteria enable us to obtain instability theorems of high co-dimensions.

•

Allowing perturbations
with finite
BV
norms
along
u=-1
with
\underline{u}>0
, in Theorem
13.2
we consider the space of initial data for
\hat{\chi}(-1,\underline{u},\omega)
and
\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)
, that can be prescribed along
u=-1
with
\underline{u}>0
. Given Christodoulou’s naked-singularity data in
[
18
]
along
\underline{u}=0
, then for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we can find smooth functions
f_{1},f_{2},...,f_{k}
and
g_{1},g_{2},...,g_{k}
with variables
\underline{u}>0
and
\omega
, such that the below prescribed initial data for
|\hat{\chi}|
and
\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi
with
\underline{u}>0

\begin{split}&\bigg(|\hat{\chi}|(-1,\underline{u},\omega),\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,\underline{u},\omega)\bigg)\\
=&\bigg(0+\lambda_{1}f_{1}+\lambda_{2}f_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}f_{k},\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)+\lambda_{1}^{\prime}g_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime}g_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}^{\prime}g_{k}\bigg)\end{split}

would lead to the trapped surface and the apparent horizon formation, when

\sum_{1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}+\sum_{1}^{k}{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}\neq 0\quad\mbox{ with }\quad\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

Moreover, if it holds

\begin{split}&\bigg(0+\lambda_{1}f_{1}+\lambda_{2}f_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}f_{k},\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)+\lambda_{1}^{\prime}g_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime}g_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\lambda_{k}^{\prime}g_{k}\bigg)\\
=&\bigg(0+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}f_{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}f_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\tilde{\lambda}_{k}f_{k},\quad\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(-1,0,\omega)+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{\prime}g_{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}^{\prime}g_{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\tilde{\lambda}_{k}^{\prime}g_{k}\bigg),\end{split}

then it renders

\lambda_{i}=\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad 1\leq i\leq k.

We may therefore say that, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, Christodoulou’s naked-singularity solution in
[
18
]
has at least co-dimensional
2k
nonlinear instability subject to outgoing BV characteristic perturbations at
(-1,0,\omega)
.

•

In Theorem
13.2
, with
m_{i}
and
m^{\prime}_{i}
being positive constants, the constructed
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
obey the requirement: for
\underline{u}>0

f_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})\geq m_{i}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ on }B_{p_{i}}(\epsilon_{i})\subset D_{i},\quad g_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})\geq m_{i}^{\prime}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ on }B_{p_{i}^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{i}^{\prime})\subset D_{i}^{\prime},

and

f_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})=0,\quad g_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})=0\quad\mbox{ for }\quad\underline{u}\leq 0.

Hence, functions
f_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})
and
g_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})
are not continuous in
\underline{u}
, when
\underline{u}\rightarrow 0^{+}
. In Section
13.2
, by exploiting the extra
\Omega
-weight gained in the hyperbolic estimates, we extend this instability theorem by allowing to choose continuous functions
f_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})
and
g_{i}(\omega,\underline{u})
for the
\underline{u}
variable with
\underline{u}\geq 0
. In Theorem
1.3
, we impose the below requirements for
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
:

\mbox{With }\,\,1\leq i\leq k,\,\,C_{1}>2,\,\,\Lambda>0\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,g(\underline{u})=({C_{1}\Lambda+C_{1}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot[\ln(\ln\frac{1}{\underline{u}})]^{-\frac{1}{2}},

we request the prescribed
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
to obey

0\leq f_{i}\leq g(\underline{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ on }\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(0,\delta],\quad f_{i}(\cdot,\underline{u})\geq m_{i}g(\underline{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ on }B_{p_{i}}(\epsilon_{i})\subset D_{i},

0\leq g_{i}\leq g(\underline{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ on }\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(0,\delta],\quad g_{i}(\cdot,\underline{u})\geq m_{i}^{\prime}g(\underline{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ on }B_{p_{i}^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{i}^{\prime})\subset D_{i}^{\prime}

with points
p_{i}\in D_{i}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
p_{i}^{\prime}\in D_{i}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
and constants
m_{i},m_{i}^{\prime}\in(0,1)
,
\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}\in(0,\pi/2)
. Note that we can set
a=1
. And as
\underline{u}\rightarrow 0^{+}
, it holds

\lim_{\underline{u}\rightarrow 0^{+}}g(\underline{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}=0.

Therefore, we can choose continuous-in-
\underline{u}
and smooth-in-
\omega
functions
f_{i},g_{i},\hat{\chi},\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi
along
u=-1
from
\underline{u}=0
to prove the corresponding high co-dimensional instability results stated in Theorem
1.3
.

1.5.
Related Results

1.5.1.
Trapped Surfaces, MOTS and Short-Pulse Method

After Christodoulou’s monumental work
[
21
]
, a systematical approach by introducing signature for the short pulse was developed by Klainerman-Rodnianski in
[
29
]
to simplify and to extend
[
21
]
in a finite region. Yu in
[
38
]
extended Klainerman-Rodnianski’s approach to the Einstein-Maxwell system. The author in
[
1
]
introduced a new signature, called the signature for decay rates, and extended Klainerman-Rodnianski’s results to past null infinity. Based on the hyperbolic estimates in
[
29
]
, Klainerman-Luk-Rodnianski in
[
28
]
found a fully anisotropic mechanism for trapped surface formation.

In
[
2
]
the author and Luk designed a new set of short-pulse hierarchies and proved the first scale-critical trapped surface formation criterion for the Einstein vacuum equations. With only the signature for decay rates, in
[
4
]
the author gave a short proof of the scale-critical trapped surface formation in the far-field regime and related the short-pulse hierarchies to the peeling properties of the gravitational waves. In
[
5
]
, the author and Athanasiou further extended the new approach in
[
4
]
to the Einstein-Maxwell system. With spherically symmetric singular initial data prescribed along the incoming null hypersurface, Li and Liu in
[
32
]
studied the Einstein-scalar field system and proved an almost scale-critical trapped surface formation criterion. Their result showed that the spherically symmetric singularities considered in
[
32
]
are unstable (as a result of trapped surface formation) subject to close-to-isotropic gravitational perturbations and the instability in
[
32
]
is in the sense of the first category, slightly weaker than co-dimension
1
. Very recently, the methods in
[
4
]
by the author and in
[
5
]
by the author and Athanasiou are also extended to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system and the Einstein-scalar field system, by Athanasiou-Mondal-Yau
[
15
]
and Zhao-Hilditch-Kroon
[
39
]
, respectively.

Based on the hyperbolic estimates in
[
2
]
, in the short-pulse regime, the MOTS along each incoming null hypersurface and the corresponding apparent horizon are constructed in
[
3
]
by the author and in
[
6
]
by the author and Han for the general anisotropic scenarios. For solving the MOTS and the apparent horizon based on spacelike foliations, interested readers are referred to
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
by Andersson, Eichmair, Mars, Metzger, Simon and references therein.

1.5.2.
Weak Cosmic Censorship within Spherical Symmetry and Naked Singularity Formation

In a series of works
[
16
]
-
[
19
]
, Christodoulou achieved the proof of weak cosmic censorship for the spherically symmetric Einstein-(real) scalar field system. After constructing the first example of the naked-singularity solution under spherical symmetry, he ingenuously obtained
4
-integrated theorems:

(1)

BV extension principle in triangle and in rectangle regions;

(2)

a sharp trapped surface formation criterion;

(3)

the first instability theorem;

(4)

the second instability theorem.

Christodoulou’s arguments in
[
16
]
-
[
19
]
rely on the special structures of the spherically symmetric Einstein-(real) scalar field system and the associated monotonicity properties. His
4
-step argument is unified. Each step is exactly matched with the other steps. In recent years, each of Christodoulou’s steps has been separately extended. In
[
8
]
the author and Lim extended
[
16
]
to the spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged (complex) scalar field system. See also the extension in
[
23
]
by Costa to the spherically symmetric Einstein-(real) scalar field system with positive cosmological constant. For the instability argument of the Einstein-(real) scalar field system, Li-Liu in
[
31
]
gave a proof with apriori estimates. Their instability theorem is slightly different from Christodoulou’s.

Extending the proof of the weak cosmic censorship to the other spherically symmetric Einstein-matter field is still very challenging. The Einstein-Maxwell-charged (complex) scalar field system is the next to be considered. However, the corresponding weak cosmic censorship has remained open since the late 1990s. Several difficulties are easily seen:

•

Due to the presence of non-constant charge
Q
, almost all previously employed monotonicity formulas fail to hold. These monotonicity formulas are critically used in Christodoulou’s BV extension/trapped surface formation/instability arguments when
Q=0
. A new strategy to incorporate non-constant charge
Q
remains to be developed.

•

For the trapped surface formation criterion, with
\eta
being the initial mass input and
\delta
being the small deformation parameter, Christodoulou needs
\eta\gtrsim\delta\log\frac{1}{\delta}
. Following Christodoulou’s method, for the charged case, in
[
8
]
the author and Lim obtained a result requiring
\eta\gtrsim\delta^{1-\frac{\omega}{2}}(>\delta\log{\frac{1}{\delta}})
with
0<\omega\ll 1
. But Christodoulou’s “blue-shift”
\gamma
in the instability argument is around
\log{\frac{1}{\delta}}
rather than
\delta^{-\frac{\omega}{2}}
. How to reconcile?

•

Christodoulou’s way of proving his instability theorems is very delicate. Can one obtain the same sharp arguments as Christodoulou did?

In a new preprint
[
9
]
, the author and Tan address these questions and we show that
the weak cosmic censorship still holds (in the sense of Christodoulou) for the gravitational collapse of the spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged (complex) scalar field system.

Recently, there is also the exciting development of constructing the naked-singularity solutions for Einstein’s field equations. Under spherical symmetry and self-similar ansatz, in
[
10
]
the author and Zhang showed naked-singularity formation for Einstein vacuum equations in higher dimensions, and in
[
25
]
Guo-Hadzic-Jang extended
[
18
]
to the Einstein-Euler system. Outside spherical symmetry, important progress has been made by Rodnianski-Shlapentokh-Rothman in
[
35
]
and by Shlapentokh-Rothman in
[
36
]
. There they prove the first naked-singularity formation result for the Einstein vacuum equations in
3+1
dimensions.

1.6.
Organizations of the Paper

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we present the settings. In Section
3
, we derive the preliminary estimates. In Section
4
, we provide the
L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})
estimates for Ricci coefficients. In Section
5
, we derive the
L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})
estimates for
the scalar field. In Section
6
, we prove energy estimates for the scalar field. In Section
7
, we establish the elliptic estimates. In Section
8
, we conduct the energy estimates for curvature components. In Section
9
, we construct the sub and super barriers for the MOTS. In Section
10
, we carry out Moser’s iteration and the Schauder estimates. In Section
11
, we demonstrate the crucial linearization and continuity arguments. In Section
12
, we obtain the existence and properties for the apparent horizon. In Section
13
, we prove the instability theorems.

1.7.
Acknowledgement

XA would like to thank Yu Deng and Willie Wong for valuable correspondences. XA is supported by MOE Tier 1 grants A-0004287-00-00, A-0008492-00-00 and MOE Tier 2 grant A-8000977-00-00.

2.
Setting

2.1.
Double Null Coordinates

In this paper, we adopt the double null coordinates
(u,\underline{u},\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
for
(\mathcal{M},g)
. For
A,B=1,2
, the spacetime metric
g
takes the form

g=-2\Omega^{2}(du\otimes d\underline{u}+d\underline{u}\otimes du)+g\mkern-9.0mu/_{AB}(d\theta^{A}-d^{A}du)\otimes(d\theta^{B}-d^{B}du).

(2.1)

Here
u
and
\underline{u}
are optical functions satisfying

g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u\partial_{\nu}u=0,\quad g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\underline{u}\partial_{\nu}\underline{u}=0.

We require both
u
and
\underline{u}
are increasing towards the future. The level sets of
u
and
\underline{u}
are called
H_{u}
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
, respectively. They are outgoing and incoming null cones. The intersections of
H_{u}
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
are topologically
2
-shpere and are denoted as
S_{u,\underline{u}}
.
In later sections, along
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
we consider the
2
-sphere
u=-R(\underline{u},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
with
R(\underline{u},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
being a given function, and for simplicity we will write this
2
-sphere as
S_{-R,\underline{u}}
.

2.2.
Equations

The equations studied here are the Einstein-scalar field system:

\begin{split}&R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu},\\
&T_{\mu\nu}=D_{\mu}\phi D_{\nu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}D^{\lambda}\phi D_{\lambda}\phi.\end{split}

Utilizing the null frame
e_{3}
,
e_{4}
and an frame
{e_{1},e_{2}}
tangent to the 2-spheres
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, with the indices
A,B\in\{1,2\}
, we define the Ricci coefficients:

\begin{split}&\chi_{AB}=g(D_{A}e_{4},e_{B}),\,\,\,\quad\underline{\chi}_{AB}=g(D_{A}e_{3},e_{B}),\\
&\eta_{A}=-\frac{1}{2}g(D_{3}e_{A},e_{4}),\quad\underline{\eta}_{A}=-\frac{1}{2}g(D_{4}e_{A},e_{3}),\\
&\hat{\omega}=-\frac{1}{4}g(D_{4}e_{3},e_{4}),\quad\,\,\,\underline{\omega}=-\frac{1}{4}g(D_{3}e_{4},e_{3}),\\
&\zeta_{A}=\frac{1}{2}g(D_{A}e_{4},e_{3}),\end{split}

(2.2)

where
D_{A}=D_{e_{A}}
.
Note that here we use
\hat{\omega}
to represent the Ricci coefficient and this is because we save
\omega
to stand for the angular variable on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We also denote
\nabla
to be the
induced covariant derivative operator on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
and let
\nabla_{3}
,
\nabla_{4}
be
the projections to
S_{u,\underline{u}}
of the covariant derivatives
D_{3}
,
D_{4}
. By above definition, it also holds

\begin{split}&\hat{\omega}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}(\log\Omega),\qquad\underline{\omega}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}(\log\Omega),\\
&\eta_{A}=\zeta_{A}+\nabla_{A}(\log\Omega),\quad\underline{\eta}_{A}=-\zeta_{A}+\nabla_{A}(\log\Omega).\end{split}

(2.3)

In
3+1
dimensions, for
i,k,l,m=1,2,3,4
we further decompose the below Weyl tensor

W_{iklm}:=R_{iklm}+\frac{1}{2}(R_{im}g_{kl}-R_{il}g_{km}+R_{kl}g_{im}-R_{km}g_{il})+\frac{1}{6}R(g_{il}g_{km}-g_{im}g_{kl})

and define the following null curvature components

\begin{split}\alpha_{AB}&=W(e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},e_{4}),\quad\,\,\,\underline{\alpha}_{AB}=W(e_{A},e_{3},e_{B},e_{3}),\\
\beta_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}W(e_{A},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\quad\underline{\beta}_{A}=\frac{1}{2}W(e_{A},e_{3},e_{3},e_{4}),\\
\rho&=\frac{1}{4}W(e_{4},e_{3},e_{4},e_{3}),\quad\sigma=\frac{1}{4}\,^{*}W(e_{4},e_{3},e_{4},e_{3}),\end{split}

(2.4)

where
\,{}^{*}W
is the Hodge dual of
W
.

We further set
\phi^{(1)}\cdot\phi^{(2)}
to stand for a contraction of the tensor product of
\phi^{(1)}
and
\phi^{(2)}
with respect to the metric
g\mkern-9.0mu/
on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
. We also denote

(\phi^{(1)}\widehat{\otimes}\phi^{(2)})_{AB}:=\phi^{(1)}_{A}\phi^{(2)}_{B}+\phi^{(1)}_{B}\phi^{(2)}_{A}-\delta_{AB}(\phi^{(1)}\cdot\phi^{(2)})\quad\mbox{for one forms $\phi^{(1)}_{A}$, $\phi^{(2)}_{A}$,}

\phi^{(1)}\wedge\phi^{(2)}:={\epsilon}\mkern-8.0mu/\,^{AB}(g\mkern-9.0mu/^{-1})^{CD}\phi^{(1)}_{AC}\phi^{(2)}_{BD}\quad\mbox{for symmetric two tensors $\phi^{(1)}_{AB}$, $\phi^{(2)}_{AB}$},

where
{\epsilon}\mkern-8.0mu/\,
is the volume form associated to the metric
g\mkern-9.0mu/
.
For totally symmetric tensors, we also employ the below
div
and
curl
operators

(\mbox{div }\phi)_{A_{1}...A_{r}}:=\nabla^{B}\phi_{BA_{1}...A_{r}},

(\mbox{curl }\phi)_{A_{1}...A_{r}}:={\epsilon}\mkern-8.0mu/\,^{BC}\nabla_{B}\phi_{CA_{1}...A_{r}}.

In addition, we define the trace to be

(\mbox{tr}\phi)_{A_{1}...A_{r-1}}:=(g\mkern-9.0mu/^{-1})^{BC}\phi_{BCA_{1}...A_{r-1}}.

Let
\hat{\chi}
and
\hat{\underline{\chi}}
be the traceless parts of
\chi
and
\underline{\chi}
respectively. Employing above notations, rewriting
R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}
according to the null frame
\{e_{3},e_{4},e_{A},e_{B}\}
with
A,B=1,2
, we have that
\chi
and
\underline{\chi}
obey the following null structure equations:

\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\mbox{tr}\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\mbox{tr}\chi)^{2}&=-|\hat{\chi}|^{2}-2\hat{\omega}\mbox{tr}\chi-D_{4}\phi D_{4}\phi,\\
\nabla_{4}\hat{\chi}_{AB}+\mbox{tr}\chi\hat{\chi}_{AB}&=-2\hat{\omega}\hat{\chi}_{AB}-\alpha_{AB},\\
\nabla_{3}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi})^{2}&=-2\underline{\omega}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}-|\hat{\underline{\chi}}|^{2}-D_{3}\phi D_{3}\phi,\\
\nabla_{3}\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}+\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\,\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}&=-2\underline{\omega}\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}-\underline{\alpha}_{AB},\\
\nabla_{4}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}&=2\hat{\omega}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+2\rho-\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+2\mbox{div }\underline{\eta}+2|\underline{\eta}|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{3}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi,\\
\nabla_{4}\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}&=(\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta})_{AB}+2\hat{\omega}\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\hat{\chi}_{AB}+(\underline{\eta}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta})_{AB}+D_{A}\phi D_{B}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{AB}D_{C}\phi D^{C}\phi,\\
\nabla_{3}\mbox{tr}\chi+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\mbox{tr}\chi&=2\underline{\omega}\mbox{tr}\chi+2\rho-\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+2\mbox{div }\eta+2|\eta|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{3}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi,\\
\nabla_{3}\hat{\chi}_{AB}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\hat{\chi}_{AB}&=(\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\eta)_{AB}+2\underline{\omega}\hat{\chi}_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}+(\eta\widehat{\otimes}\eta)_{AB}+D_{A}\phi D_{B}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{AB}D_{C}\phi D^{C}\phi.\end{split}

(2.5)

And the Ricci coefficients
\eta_{A},\underline{\eta}_{A},\underline{\omega},\hat{\omega}
obey the remaining null structure equations:

\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\eta_{A}&=-\chi_{AB}\cdot(\eta-\underline{\eta})_{B}-\beta_{A}-\frac{1}{2}D_{A}\phi D_{4}\phi,\\
\nabla_{3}\underline{\eta}_{A}&=-\underline{\chi}_{AB}\cdot(\underline{\eta}-\eta)_{B}+\underline{\beta}_{A}-\frac{1}{2}D_{A}\phi D_{3}\phi,\\
\nabla_{4}\underline{\omega}&=2\hat{\omega}\underline{\omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\eta_{A}\cdot\underline{\eta}^{A}+\frac{1}{2}|\eta|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho+\frac{1}{6}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{12}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi,\\
\nabla_{3}\hat{\omega}&=2\hat{\omega}\underline{\omega}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta_{A}\cdot\underline{\eta}^{A}+\frac{1}{2}|\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho+\frac{1}{6}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{12}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi.\end{split}

(2.6)

On
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, with the null frame, the Gauss-Codazzi equations boil down to the constraint equations:

\begin{split}\mbox{div }\hat{\chi}_{A}=&\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}\mbox{tr}\chi-\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{B}-\underline{\eta}_{B})\cdot(\hat{\chi}_{BA}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi g_{BA})-\beta_{A}+\frac{1}{2}D_{A}\phi D_{4}\phi,\\
\mbox{div }\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{A}=&\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{B}-\underline{\eta}_{B})\cdot(\hat{\underline{\chi}}_{BA}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}g_{BA})+\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}D_{A}\phi D_{3}\phi,\\
\mbox{curl }\eta=&-\mbox{curl }\underline{\eta}=\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\wedge\hat{\chi},\\
K=&-(\rho+\frac{1}{6}R)-\frac{1}{4}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}g^{AB}D_{A}\phi D_{B}\phi\\
=&-\rho-\frac{1}{4}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{6}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{3}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi.\end{split}

(2.7)

Here
K
is the Gauss curvature of the spheres
S_{u,\underline{u}}
.

For the system (
1.1
), the scalar field
\phi
obeys
\Box_{g}\phi=0
. And we rewrite the wave equation as a transport equation

\begin{split}0=\Box_{g}\phi=&g^{\lambda\mu}D_{\lambda}D_{\mu}\phi=g^{\lambda\mu}D_{\lambda}(D_{\mu}\phi)-g^{\lambda\mu}D_{D_{\lambda}e_{\mu}}\phi\\
=&g^{34}D_{3}(D_{4}\phi)-g^{34}D_{D_{3}e_{4}}\phi+g^{43}D_{4}(D_{3}\phi)-g^{43}D_{D_{4}e_{3}}\phi+g^{AB}D_{A}(D_{B}\phi)-g^{AB}D_{D_{A}e_{B}}\phi\\
=&-\frac{1}{2}D_{3}D_{4}\phi-\frac{1}{2}D_{4}D_{3}\phi+g^{AB}D_{A}(D_{B}\phi)-g^{AB}D_{\nabla_{A}e_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\chi_{AB}e_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\chi}_{AB}e_{4}}\phi\\
=&-D_{3}D_{4}\phi+\Delta_{g}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi e_{3}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}e_{4}\phi.\end{split}

It hence holds

D_{3}D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi D_{3}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}D_{4}\phi=g^{AB}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\phi.

(2.8)

For convenience, we also employ the following two equivalent forms
(see Section
6
)

(\Omega e_{3})(\Omega e_{4}\phi)+\frac{1}{2}{\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}\Omega e_{4}\phi=\Omega^{2}\Delta_{g}\phi-\frac{1}{2}{\Omega\mbox{tr}\chi}\Omega e_{3}\phi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2\Omega^{2}\eta^{A}e_{A}\phi},

(2.9)

(\Omega e_{4})(\Omega e_{3}\phi)+\frac{1}{2}{\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}}\Omega e_{4}\phi=\Omega^{2}\Delta_{g}\phi-\frac{1}{2}{\Omega\mbox{tr}\chi}\Omega e_{3}\phi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2\Omega^{2}\underline{\eta}^{A}e_{A}\phi}.

(2.10)

Using the property of covariant derivatives, the scalar field
\phi
also satisfies

\begin{split}\nabla_{\Omega e_{3}}\nabla_{A}\phi=&e_{A}(\Omega e_{3}\phi)-\Omega{\underline{\chi}_{A}}^{B}e_{B}\phi,\\
\nabla_{\Omega e_{4}}\nabla_{A}\phi=&e_{A}(\Omega e_{4}\phi)-\Omega{\chi_{A}}^{B}e_{B}\phi.\end{split}

(2.11)

Recall that in
3+1
dimensions for
i,k,l,m=1,2,3,4
, the Weyl curvatures obey

\begin{split}\nabla^{i}W_{iklm}=&-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{m}R_{lk}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{l}R_{mk}-\frac{1}{12}(\nabla_{l}Rg_{mk}-\nabla_{m}Rg_{lk}).\end{split}

(2.12)

Employing the null frame
\{e_{3},e_{4},e_{A},e_{B}\}
with
A,B=1,2
, we rewrite the above (
2.12
) as the below null Bianchi equations:

\begin{split}&\nabla_{3}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\alpha=\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\beta+4\underline{\omega}\alpha-3(\hat{\chi}\rho+^{*}\hat{\chi}\sigma)+(\zeta+4\eta)\widehat{\otimes}\beta+\frac{1}{4}(D_{3}R_{44}-D_{4}R_{43})g_{AB}+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{4}Rg_{AB},\\
&\nabla_{4}\beta+2\mbox{tr}\chi\beta=\mbox{div }\alpha-2\hat{\omega}\beta+\eta\cdot\alpha-\frac{1}{2}(D_{A}R_{44}-D_{4}R_{4A}),\\
&\nabla_{3}\beta+\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\beta=\nabla\rho+2\underline{\omega}\beta+^{*}\nabla\sigma+2\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\beta}+3(\eta\rho+^{*}\eta\sigma)+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}(g^{CD}R_{CD})-\nabla^{B}R_{BA}+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{A}R+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}R_{4A}},\\
&\nabla_{4}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\sigma=-\mbox{div }^{*}\beta+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot^{*}\alpha-\zeta\cdot^{*}\beta-2\underline{\eta}\cdot^{*}\beta-\frac{1}{4}(D_{\mu}R_{4\nu}-D_{\nu}R_{4\mu}){\epsilon^{\mu\nu}}_{34},\\
&\nabla_{3}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\sigma=-\mbox{div }^{*}\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot^{*}\underline{\alpha}-\zeta\cdot^{*}\underline{\beta}-2\eta\cdot^{*}\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{4}(D_{\mu}R_{3\nu}-D_{\nu}R_{3\mu}){\epsilon^{\mu\nu}}_{34},\\
&\nabla_{4}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\rho=\mbox{div }\beta-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\alpha+\zeta\cdot\beta+2\underline{\eta}\cdot\beta-\frac{1}{4}(D_{3}R_{44}-D_{4}R_{43})+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{4}R,\\
&\nabla_{3}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\rho=-\mbox{div }\underline{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\zeta\cdot\underline{\beta}-2\eta\cdot\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{4}(D_{3}R_{34}-D_{4}R_{33})+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{3}R,\\
&\nabla_{4}\underline{\beta}+\mbox{tr}\chi\underline{\beta}=-\nabla\rho+^{*}\nabla\sigma+2\hat{\omega}\underline{\beta}+2\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\beta-3(\underline{\eta}\rho-^{*}\underline{\eta}\sigma){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}(g^{CD}R_{CD})+\nabla^{B}R_{BA}-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{A}R-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}R_{3A}},\\
\ &\nabla_{3}\underline{\beta}+2\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\,\underline{\beta}=-\mbox{div }\underline{\alpha}-2\underline{\omega}\underline{\beta}+\underline{\eta}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}(D_{A}R_{33}-D_{3}R_{3A}),\\
&\nabla_{4}\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\underline{\alpha}=-\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}+4\hat{\omega}\underline{\alpha}-3(\hat{\underline{\chi}}\rho-^{*}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\sigma)+(\zeta-4\underline{\eta})\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{4}(D_{4}R_{33}-D_{3}R_{34})g_{AB}+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{3}Rg_{AB}.\end{split}

(2.13)

Here,
∗
denotes the Hodge dual on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
.

In our proof, we utilize the null Bianchi equations to deduce energy estimates. We will employ the renormalized quantities
4
4

4

For more general incoming initial data, the renormalized quantity for
K
is
K-K(u,0)-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi
.

\begin{split}&\beta_{A}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi,\quad K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi,\\
&\check{\sigma}=\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\wedge\hat{\underline{\chi}},\quad\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi.\end{split}

These renormalized quantities satisfy the below renormalized null Bianchi equations:

\begin{split}\nabla_{3}(\beta_{\cdot}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}\phi\nabla_{\cdot}\phi)_{A}+\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\beta_{A}=&-\nabla K+^{*}\nabla\check{\sigma}+2\underline{\omega}\beta+2\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\beta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-3(\eta K\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-^{*}\eta\check{\sigma})\\
&+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla(\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}})-^{*}\nabla(\hat{\chi}\wedge\hat{\underline{\chi}}))-\frac{3}{4}\eta\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\\
&+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{3}{2}}(\eta\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}-^{*}\eta\hat{\chi}\wedge\hat{\underline{\chi}})-\frac{1}{4}(\nabla\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\mbox{tr}\chi\nabla\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi})\\
&+\nabla_{A}(g^{CD}R_{CD})-\nabla^{B}R_{BA}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{A}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla_{4}\phi+\eta_{A}\nabla_{C}\phi\nabla^{C}\phi.}\end{split}

(2.14)

\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\check{\sigma}+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\check{\sigma}=&-\mbox{div }^{*}(\beta-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{4}\phi\nabla\phi)-\zeta\cdot^{*}\beta-2\underline{\eta}\cdot^{*}\beta+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot^{*}(\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta})+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot^{*}(\underline{\eta}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta})\\
&-\nabla_{1}R_{42}+\nabla_{2}R_{41}+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{A}\phi\nabla_{B}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{AB}\nabla_{C}\phi\nabla^{C}\phi)\wedge\hat{\chi}^{AB},\\
\end{split}

(2.15)

\begin{split}&\nabla_{4}(K-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)+\mbox{tr}\chi(K-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)\\
=&-\mbox{div }\beta-\zeta\cdot\beta-2\underline{\eta}\cdot\beta+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot(\underline{\eta}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta})\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{div }\underline{\eta}-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\chi|\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\eta}^{A}\nabla_{A}\phi\nabla_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g}\phi\nabla_{4}\phi\\
&+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{A}\phi\nabla_{B}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{AB}\nabla_{C}\phi\nabla^{C}\phi)\cdot\hat{\chi}^{AB},\\
\end{split}

(2.16)

\begin{split}\nabla_{3}\check{\sigma}+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\check{\sigma}=&-\mbox{div }^{*}(\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla\phi)-\zeta\cdot^{*}\underline{\beta}-2\eta\cdot^{*}\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot^{*}(\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\eta)+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot^{*}(\eta\widehat{\otimes}\eta)\\
&+\nabla_{1}R_{32}-\nabla_{2}R_{31}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}^{AB}\wedge(\nabla_{A}\phi\nabla_{B}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{AB}\nabla_{C}\phi\nabla^{C}\phi),\\
\end{split}

(2.17)

\begin{split}&\nabla_{3}(K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)+\frac{3}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}(K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)\\
=&\mbox{div }(\underline{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{A}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi-\zeta\cdot\underline{\beta}+2\eta\cdot\underline{\beta}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\eta+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot(\eta\widehat{\otimes}\eta)-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}|\eta|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{A}\nabla_{A}\phi\nabla_{3}\phi\\
&+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{A}\phi\nabla_{B}\phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{AB}\nabla_{C}\phi\nabla^{C}\phi)\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}^{AB}-\frac{1}{8}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}(-\mbox{div }\eta+K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)-\frac{\Omega^{-1}}{|u|^{2}}(\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{2}{|u|}),\end{split}

(2.18)

\begin{split}\nabla_{4}(\underline{\beta}_{\cdot}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla_{\cdot}\phi)_{A}+\mbox{tr}\chi\underline{\beta}_{A}=&\nabla(K-\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi)+^{*}\nabla\check{\sigma}+2\omega\underline{\beta}+2\hat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\beta+3(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\underline{\eta}K+^{*}\underline{\eta}\check{\sigma})\\
&+\nabla(-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{A}\phi\nabla_{A}\phi+\frac{1}{4}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}})-\frac{1}{2}{{}^{*}\nabla}(\hat{\chi}\wedge\hat{\underline{\chi}})\\
&+\nabla_{A}(g^{CD}R_{CD})+\nabla^{B}R_{BA}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\frac{1}{2}\underline{\eta}_{A}\nabla_{3}\phi\nabla_{4}\phi-\underline{\eta}_{A}\nabla_{C}\phi\nabla^{C}\phi.}\end{split}

(2.19)

Remark 10
.

In below, if no further clarification, we employ the capital Latin letters
A\in\{1,2\}
as frame indices on the spheres
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, and use Greek letters
\mu\in\{1,2,3,4\}
as frame indices in the whole 3+1 dimensional spacetime.

2.3.
Naked Singularity Initial Data

In
[
18
]
, Christodoulou constructed a spherically-symmetric naked-singularity spacetime. It is portrayed in below picture.

Naked Singularity
r=0
u=-1
O\,\,\,\,
\underline{u}=0
u=0

Anisotropic Apparent Horizon
r=0
u=-1
O\,\,\,\,
\underline{u}=0
\underline{u}=\delta
Anisotropic Perturbation

Christodoulou’s solution solves the Einstein-scalar field system
1.1
. In
[
18
]
a spacetime singularity forms in gravitational collapse and the whole spacetime is free of trapped surfaces and the apparent horizon.

In this article, we consider a characteristic initial value problem with data prescribed along
\underline{u}=0
with
-1\leq u\leq 0
and
u=-1
with
0\leq\underline{u}\leq\delta
. For
\underline{u}\leq 0
, we adopt the spacetime
(\mathcal{M}_{int},g_{int})
constructed in
[
18
]
with
\mathcal{M}_{int}
standing for the interior spacetime region
\underline{u}\leq 0
.

\mbox{Setting }v=4\underline{u},\mbox{ we let }g_{int}=-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{2}(u,v)(du\otimes dv+dv\otimes du)+r^{2}(u,v)[(d\theta^{1})^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta^{1}(d\theta^{2})^{2}].

Here
r(u,v)
is the radius function.

We will add general (anisotropic) perturbation along
u=-1
starting from
\underline{u}=0
and for
\underline{u}\geq 0
the spacetime metric
g
is of the form (
2.1
). In particular, along
\underline{u}=0
, we prescribe Christodoulou’s spherically-symmetric naked-singularity initial data: via Christodoulou’s construction in
[
18
]
, for Hawking mass

m=\frac{r}{2}(1+4\Omega^{-2}\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r)
, it holds that

\frac{2m}{r}(u,0)=\mu^{*}\mbox{ with }\mu^{*}\mbox{ being a positive constant less than }1.

We also set

r(-1,0)=1,\quad\partial_{v}r(-1,0)=\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{4}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\partial_{u}r(u,0)=-1.

Since along
\underline{u}=0
it holds
\mu^{*}=\frac{2m}{r}=1+4\Omega^{-2}\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r
, we get

\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}r(u,0)=\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{4}\quad\mbox{along}\quad\underline{u}=0.

Employing the equation
5
5

5

It is equivalent to
r\partial_{u}\partial_{v}r=-\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{2}
.

\partial_{u}[\ln(\partial_{v}r)]=\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\cdot\frac{\frac{2m}{r}}{1-\frac{2m}{r}},

with
r(-1,0)=1
and
\partial_{v}r(-1,0)=(1-\mu^{*})/4
, we obtain

\partial_{v}r(u,0)=\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{4}r^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}(u,0)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\Omega^{2}(u,0)=r^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}(u,0).

Using
v=4\underline{u}
, these further imply

\partial_{\underline{u}}r(u,0)=(1-\mu^{*})\cdot r^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}(u,0)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\Omega^{-2}\partial_{\underline{u}}r(u,0)=1-\mu^{*}.

Recall
\mbox{tr}\chi(u,0)=g^{AB}g(D_{A}e_{4},e_{B})(u,0)
,
\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}(u,0)=g^{AB}g(D_{A}e_{3},e_{B})(u,0)
and along
\underline{u}=0
it holds
e_{3}=\Omega^{-1}\partial_{u},e_{4}=\Omega^{-1}\partial_{\underline{u}}
. We hence derive

\Omega^{-1}\mbox{tr}\chi(u,0)=\Omega^{-1}g^{AB}\cdot\Gamma^{D}_{A4}\cdot g_{BD}=\frac{2}{r}\Omega^{-2}\partial_{\underline{u}}r(u,0)=\frac{2(1-\mu^{*})}{r(u,0)},

\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}(u,0)=\Omega g^{AB}\cdot\Gamma^{D}_{A3}\cdot g_{BD}=\frac{2}{r}\partial_{u}r(u,0)=-\frac{2}{r(u,0)}.

Utilizing
\underline{\omega}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\log\Omega
, we also have

\Omega\underline{\omega}(u,0)=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{u}\log\Omega(u,0)=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{u}\log[r^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{2(1-\mu^{*})}}(u,0)]=\frac{\mu^{*}}{4(1-\mu^{*})}\cdot\frac{1}{r(u,0)}.

(2.20)

We proceed to calculate the initial value of
K
along
\underline{u}=0
. Applying the below null structure equation and the Gauss equation

\begin{split}\nabla_{3}\mbox{tr}\chi+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}\mbox{tr}\chi=&2\underline{\omega}\mbox{tr}\chi+2\rho-\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+2\mbox{div }\eta+2|\eta|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{3}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi,\\
K=&-\rho-\frac{1}{4}\mbox{tr}\chi\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\hat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{6}D_{3}\phi D_{4}\phi+\frac{1}{3}D_{A}\phi D^{A}\phi,\end{split}

further employing
\underline{\omega}=-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{-1}\nabla_{3}\Omega
and restricting the equations to
\underline{u}=0
, we obtain

2\Omega^{2}K(u,0)+\partial_{u}(\Omega^{2}\cdot\Omega^{-1}\mbox{tr}\chi)(u,0)+\Omega^{2}\cdot\Omega^{-1}\mbox{tr}\chi\cdot\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}(u,0)=0.

Together with the fact

\Omega^{-1}\mbox{tr}\chi(u,0)=\frac{2(1-\mu^{*})}{r(u,0)},\quad\Omega\mbox{tr}\underline{\chi}(u,0)=-\frac{2}{r(u,0)},\quad\Omega^{2}(u,0)=r^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}(u,0),\quad\partial_{u}r(u,0)=-1,

we deduce

2K(u,0)+r^{-\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}\cdot\partial_{u}(r^{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}-1})\cdot 2(1-\mu^{*})(u,0)-\frac{4(1-\mu^{*})}{r(u,0)^{2}}=0,

which infers

K(u,0)=-(2-4\mu^{*}-4+4\mu^{*})\cdot\frac{1}{2r(u,0)^{2}}=\frac{1}{r(u,0)^{2}}.

Quoting the results in
[
18
]
, for the scalar field along
\underline{u}=0
it holds

\begin{split}\Omega\partial_{3}\phi(u,0)=&\partial_{u}\phi(u,0)=\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}\frac{1}{r(u,0)},\\
\Omega\partial_{4}\phi(u,0)=&\partial_{\underline{u}}\phi(u,0)=\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{1-\mu^{*}}{\mu^{*}}}\cdot\frac{\partial_{\underline{u}}r(u,0)}{r(u,0)}.\end{split}

(2.21)

Remark 11
.

Along
\underline{u}=0
, employing
\partial_{u}r(u,0)=-1
and

\partial_{u}(\Omega^{-2}\partial_{u}r)=-\frac{1}{2}r\Omega^{-2}(\partial_{u}\phi)^{2},

via (
2.21
) we also obtain

\Omega\underline{\omega}(u,0)=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{u}\log\Omega(u,0)=\frac{|r\partial_{u}\phi|^{2}}{8r}(u,0)=\frac{\mu^{*}}{4(1-\mu^{*})}\cdot\frac{1}{r(u,0)},

which is consistent with (
2.20
).

Remark 12
.

The
\sqrt{2}
on the right of (
2.21
) is owing to in
[
18
]
Christodoulou’s use of

\begin{split}&\mbox{Ric}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=2T_{\mu\nu},\\
&T_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\phi_{c}\partial_{\nu}\phi_{c}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\sigma}\phi_{c}\partial_{\sigma}\phi_{c}.\end{split}

Here we denote
\phi_{c}
to be the scalar field function employed in
[
18
]
. Denoting
k:=\sqrt{\frac{\mu^{*}}{1-\mu^{*}}}
, on page 644 of
[
18
]
, we have that along
s=s_{*}
(i.e.
\underline{u}=0
) it holds
